I’ll have a few minutes where I’m going over one thing, then I move on, then it pops back in again, and I’m mentally reviewing it while I’m trying to do something else.\n\nOn a typical day, I’d say maybe… I don’t know, maybe half an hour total? Sometimes more if it’s a busy day or if there were a lot of details to keep track of. It can feel like more than that because it keeps interrupting me. Like I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already did that,” but then the thought keeps looping back anyway. They do feel unwanted, in the sense that I’m not sitting there choosing to think about these things for fun. I’ll be trying to get through my day, and then suddenly I’m worrying about a work schedule, or whether I paid a bill, or whether I made some little paperwork error that’s going to come back and cause a bigger problem.\n\nBut at the same time, they don’t always feel completely random, if that makes sense. It’s usually tied to something real, like a bill or a travel plan or a detail at work. It’s just that my mind takes an ordinary thing and treats it like it could turn into some much larger mess. Like one small mix-up won’t just be inconvenient—it’ll somehow snowball. More replaying the meaning of it, if that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you go over these details in your mind, does it feel like something you have to do to relieve the anxiety?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 44 / receptionist_or_information_clerk / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Uncontrollable_Worry\nmanifestation: \"I can tell myself, ‘Sunny, it’s handled,’ but the thought keeps looping, and pretty soon I’ve gone from one concern to another until I’m checking details in my head over and over.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… it does feel like I have to do it, even when I know that doesn’t totally make sense. It’s like if I don’t go over the details, the anxiety just sits there and keeps poking at me. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already dealt with it,” but the thought doesn’t really listen to that. It keeps looping back around.

And then I’ll start with one thing, like, “Did I send that message correctly?” or “Did I remember that appointment?” and before I know it, I’m onto another concern and then another one. It’s almost like my brain starts making a little list without me meaning to. So I end up checking details in my head over and over, trying to get that feeling of certainty.

It does relieve the anxiety a little bit, but only for a short time. Then the doubt creeps back in, and I feel pulled to review it again. That’s the part that bothers me, because I’m not doing it because I want to sit there thinking about all these things. I’m doing it because it feels like I won’t be able to relax unless I do.

---------- turn 12 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=gpt-5.4 ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.9, F43.22, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess what finally made me make the appointment is that I’ve been having more trouble keeping my place with normal work things. I’m a receptionist, so I’m scheduling appointments and keeping track of little details all day, and lately I’ll be in the middle of entering something or arranging the lobby displays, and it’s like my mind just slips off track.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t know how to do the work or anything. I’ll start thinking, “Did I forget to tell someone something?” or “What if I overlooked that note?” and then I lose where I was. Then I have to back up and check again, sometimes more than once, and it’s just been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts like “Did I forget to tell someone something?” pop up, do they feel intrusive and hard to dismiss?\n\nPatient: Yeah… intrusive is a good word for it, I think. It’s like I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already did that,” and for maybe a second I believe it, but then the thought just sort of comes back around again. Like it doesn’t feel settled.\n\nAnd it’s not always just the one thing, either. I’ll start with, “Did I pass that message along?” and then it turns into, “What if I put the appointment in the wrong place?” or “What if I missed some little detail and someone’s going to be upset later?” And then I’m mentally going back through the steps, almost replaying them, checking them in my head over and over. I know it’s not really helping after a certain point, but it’s hard to just drop it and move on.\n\nDoctor: When those doubts come up, do you find yourself checking your work again and again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, but it’s a little tricky, because sometimes it’s not even that I physically go back and check the computer or the paper every single time. Sometimes I do that, definitely, like I’ll re-open the appointment or look back at a note I already know I read. But a lot of the time it’s more like I’m checking it in my head over and over.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already did that,” and I’ll try to move on, but the thought doesn’t really stay put. It loops back. And then I start going from one thing to the next, like first I’m worried I didn’t enter something correctly, then I’m thinking maybe I forgot to pass along a message, and then I’m remembering some tiny detail from earlier and wondering if I missed that too.\n\nSo yes, I guess I am checking again and again, just sometimes it’s mental checking more than anything. And it feels frustrating because I know after a point I’m not actually finding anything new, I’m just trying to get that settled feeling, and I don’t really get it.\n\nDoctor: When that unsettled feeling shows up, do you feel like you have to keep checking until it feels completely certain?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s pretty close to what happens. It’s not like I sit there thinking, “I need absolute certainty,” in those words, but that’s kind of what I’m reaching for. I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already did that,” and I do mean it in the moment. I’m trying to be reasonable with myself.\n\nBut then the thought doesn’t really stay gone. It loops back around, and then it’s like I’m not just checking the original thing anymore. I start with one concern, like whether I put something in correctly, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I passed along a message, or whether I missed some little note, or whether there was another detail I should have caught. And before I know it, I’m going through all these steps in my head over and over.\n\nI think I’m trying to get to that feeling of, “Okay, yes, everything is definitely fine,” but I don’t always get there. Or if I do, it doesn’t last very long. It’s frustrating, because part of me knows I’ve already checked, or at least thought it through, but it still feels unsettled enough that I keep circling back.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeated doubts feel unreasonable to you, even while you’re having them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, a lot of the time I can tell it’s probably unreasonable, or at least bigger than the situation really calls for. Like, logically I know a small paperwork mistake at work is usually just a small mistake. Or if a schedule gets a little mixed up, it can usually be fixed. But in my head it starts feeling like, “Well, what if this turns into a whole bigger problem?” Like one little thing could snowball somehow.\n\nAnd it’s not just work, honestly. I catch myself doing it with ordinary things too—bills, travel plans, schedules, little details that most people would probably check once and move on from. I’ll be thinking, “Did I pay that on time?” or “What if I misunderstood the plan?” or “What if I wrote something down wrong?” And even while I’m thinking it, there’s this part of me that knows, Sunny, you’re making this heavier than it needs to be. But that doesn’t always make it stop.\n\nSo yes, I do see that the doubts are often not… I don’t know, not proportionate, I guess. But in the moment they still feel important enough that I can’t quite let them go.\n\nDoctor: About how much time do these checking thoughts and mental reviewing take up in a typical day?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to put an exact number on it, because it’s not always like I sit down and check for one solid hour or something. It’s more broken up through the day. I’ll have a few minutes where I’m going over one thing, then I move on, then it pops back in again, and I’m mentally reviewing it while I’m trying to do something else.\n\nOn a typical day, I’d say maybe… I don’t know, maybe half an hour total? Sometimes more if it’s a busy day or if there were a lot of details to keep track of. It can feel like more than that because it keeps interrupting me. Like I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already did that,” but then the thought keeps looping back anyway. And then pretty soon I’ve gone from one concern to another, and I’m checking details in my head over and over.\n\nSo it’s not all day, exactly. I can still do my work and get through things. But it does take up enough time and attention that I notice it, and it wears on me.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts feel like unwanted intrusions, rather than the kind of worries you see as justified by real-life problems?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes and no. They do feel unwanted, in the sense that I’m not sitting there choosing to think about these things for fun. I’ll be trying to get through my day, and then suddenly I’m worrying about a work schedule, or whether I paid a bill, or whether I made some little paperwork error that’s going to come back and cause a bigger problem.\n\nBut at the same time, they don’t always feel completely random, if that makes sense. It’s usually tied to something real, like a bill or a travel plan or a detail at work. It’s just that my mind takes an ordinary thing and treats it like it could turn into some much larger mess. Like one small mix-up won’t just be inconvenient—it’ll somehow snowball. So part of me feels like, “Well, I’m being responsible, I should think about this,” and another part of me knows I’m going way past what the situation probably needs.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say they feel justified exactly. They feel like regular life worries that get too big and too sticky, and then I can’t quite put them down.\n\nDoctor: Besides going over things in your mind, do you also find yourself repeatedly checking things in a physical way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say repeatedly in a big way. I mean, I do normal checking, like making sure the door is locked or looking at an email before I send it, but I don’t think I’m going back over and over physically the way I do in my head.\n\nMost of it is more mental for me. Like I might remember that I already looked at something, but then I’ll keep replaying whether I looked carefully enough, if that makes sense. I’m not usually standing there checking the stove ten times or anything like that. It’s more that I’ll review it internally and sort of try to reassure myself that it was handled.\n\nI guess if I’m really unsure, I might look once more—like checking a bill payment confirmation or a calendar entry—but I don’t think it’s a major physical checking routine. It’s more the thinking afterward that gets sticky.\n\nDoctor: If you try not to go over it again in your mind, what does it feel like will happen?\n\nPatient: It feels like… if I don’t go over it, then I’m leaving something unfinished, even if I technically know I already handled it. Like there’s this uncomfortable loose end in my mind. I can tell myself, “Sunny, you already checked that, it’s fine, move on,” but it doesn’t really settle. The thought just sort of comes back around, like, “Are you sure? What if you missed one detail?”\n\nAnd then if I try to ignore it, I feel like I’m taking a risk, even though I know that sounds a little dramatic for the kinds of things I’m talking about. It’s not usually some huge crisis. It’s more like a schedule, a bill, a message, a small work detail. But my mind acts like if I don’t review it again, something could slip through and then I’ll have to deal with the consequences later.\n\nThe frustrating part is that it doesn’t stay with just one thing. I’ll start by trying not to think about one concern, and then another one pops up, and pretty soon I’m mentally checking several things. So even when I know it’s handled, the thought keeps looping, and I end up going over details in my head again just to try to feel sure.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries and mental checking start after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it did start after something specific. It’s been about nine months now, give or take, and it really seemed to begin after that overnight detention and the whole paperwork mix-up around it.\n\nI don’t know if I connected it right away, because at first I think I was just telling myself, “Well, of course I’m stressed, that was a bad experience,” and I was trying to just get back to normal. But when I look back, that’s when this pattern of second-guessing and going back over details really started to feel more constant. Before that, I could worry, sure, but it didn’t have this same… sticky quality to it.\n\nAnd the paperwork part of it especially, I think, made me feel like one mistake or one missing detail could turn into something much bigger than it should. So now when anything involves forms, schedules, bills, confirmations—anything where there’s a record or some official detail—I notice my mind goes right back to that feeling. But yes, the short answer is that it’s been going on for about nine months, since that incident and the paperwork mix-up connected to it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts ever feel like they pull you back into that detention incident, as if you’re reliving it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as reliving it, exactly. I mean, I do think about it, and sometimes the worry sort of points back to it, especially if something reminds me of paperwork or making sure something is documented correctly. But it’s more like I’m worrying about making another mistake or missing something, not like I’m suddenly back there in the moment.\n\nI don’t usually feel like I’m physically in that situation again, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that the memory kind of sits in the background and gives the current worry more weight. Like my mind says, “Remember what happened when something wasn’t handled right?” But I wouldn’t say I’m seeing it happen again or feeling like I’m actually there.\n\nSo… no, not really reliving it. More replaying the meaning of it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When you go over these details in your mind, does it feel like something you have to do to relieve the anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does feel like I have to do it, even when I know that doesn’t totally make sense. It’s like if I don’t go over the details, the anxiety just sits there and keeps poking at me. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already dealt with it,” but the thought doesn’t really listen to that. It keeps looping back around.\n\nAnd then I’ll start with one thing, like, “Did I send that message correctly?” or “Did I remember that appointment?” and before I know it, I’m onto another concern and then another one. It’s almost like my brain starts making a little list without me meaning to. So I end up checking details in my head over and over, trying to get that feeling of certainty.\n\nIt does relieve the anxiety a little bit, but only for a short time. Then the doubt creeps back in, and I feel pulled to review it again. That’s the part that bothers me, because I’m not doing it because I want to sit there thinking about all these things. I’m doing it because it feels like I won’t be able to relax unless I do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’m not sure I’d say I avoid reminders in a really clear way. I mean… I don’t think I’m going out of my way to avoid anything connected to it, at least not that I’ve noticed. Like, logically I know a small paperwork mistake at work is usually just a small mistake. Or if a schedule gets a little mixed up, it can usually be fixed. Sometimes more if it’s a busy day or if there were a lot of details to keep track of. It can feel like more than that because it keeps interrupting me. I’m not usually standing there checking the stove ten times or anything like that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, part of me knows it’s excessive. I can kind of hear myself thinking, Sunny, this is handled, you already checked it, you don’t need to keep going over it. But the problem is that knowing that doesn’t make the thought stop.

It’s like there’s the reasonable part of me that can see I’m circling, and then there’s the anxious part that keeps saying, But what if you missed something? What if you’re remembering it wrong? So I’ll try to move on, but then the thought loops right back around, and pretty soon I’ve gone from one concern to another. I might start out checking one detail in my head, and then suddenly I’m reviewing three or four other things too.

So yes, I do recognize that it doesn’t really make sense to keep doing it as much as I do. It just feels hard to stop once it gets going, because the checking feels like the only way to calm it down, even if only for a little while.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.9, F43.22, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess what finally made me make the appointment is that I’ve been having more trouble keeping my place with normal work things. I’m a receptionist, so I’m scheduling appointments and keeping track of little details all day, and lately I’ll be in the middle of entering something or arranging the lobby displays, and it’s like my mind just slips off track.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t know how to do the work or anything. I’ll start thinking, “Did I forget to tell someone something?” or “What if I overlooked that note?” and then I lose where I was. Then I have to back up and check again, sometimes more than once, and it’s just been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts like “Did I forget to tell someone something?” pop up, do they feel intrusive and hard to dismiss?\n\nPatient: Yeah… intrusive is a good word for it, I think. It’s like I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already did that,” and for maybe a second I believe it, but then the thought just sort of comes back around again. Like it doesn’t feel settled.\n\nAnd it’s not always just the one thing, either. I’ll start with, “Did I pass that message along?” and then it turns into, “What if I put the appointment in the wrong place?” or “What if I missed some little detail and someone’s going to be upset later?” And then I’m mentally going back through the steps, almost replaying them, checking them in my head over and over. I know it’s not really helping after a certain point, but it’s hard to just drop it and move on.\n\nDoctor: When those doubts come up, do you find yourself checking your work again and again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, but it’s a little tricky, because sometimes it’s not even that I physically go back and check the computer or the paper every single time. Sometimes I do that, definitely, like I’ll re-open the appointment or look back at a note I already know I read. But a lot of the time it’s more like I’m checking it in my head over and over.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already did that,” and I’ll try to move on, but the thought doesn’t really stay put. It loops back. And then I start going from one thing to the next, like first I’m worried I didn’t enter something correctly, then I’m thinking maybe I forgot to pass along a message, and then I’m remembering some tiny detail from earlier and wondering if I missed that too.\n\nSo yes, I guess I am checking again and again, just sometimes it’s mental checking more than anything. And it feels frustrating because I know after a point I’m not actually finding anything new, I’m just trying to get that settled feeling, and I don’t really get it.\n\nDoctor: When that unsettled feeling shows up, do you feel like you have to keep checking until it feels completely certain?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s pretty close to what happens. It’s not like I sit there thinking, “I need absolute certainty,” in those words, but that’s kind of what I’m reaching for. I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already did that,” and I do mean it in the moment. I’m trying to be reasonable with myself.\n\nBut then the thought doesn’t really stay gone. It loops back around, and then it’s like I’m not just checking the original thing anymore. I start with one concern, like whether I put something in correctly, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I passed along a message, or whether I missed some little note, or whether there was another detail I should have caught. And before I know it, I’m going through all these steps in my head over and over.\n\nI think I’m trying to get to that feeling of, “Okay, yes, everything is definitely fine,” but I don’t always get there. Or if I do, it doesn’t last very long. It’s frustrating, because part of me knows I’ve already checked, or at least thought it through, but it still feels unsettled enough that I keep circling back.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeated doubts feel unreasonable to you, even while you’re having them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, a lot of the time I can tell it’s probably unreasonable, or at least bigger than the situation really calls for. Like, logically I know a small paperwork mistake at work is usually just a small mistake. Or if a schedule gets a little mixed up, it can usually be fixed. But in my head it starts feeling like, “Well, what if this turns into a whole bigger problem?” Like one little thing could snowball somehow.\n\nAnd it’s not just work, honestly. I catch myself doing it with ordinary things too—bills, travel plans, schedules, little details that most people would probably check once and move on from. I’ll be thinking, “Did I pay that on time?” or “What if I misunderstood the plan?” or “What if I wrote something down wrong?” And even while I’m thinking it, there’s this part of me that knows, Sunny, you’re making this heavier than it needs to be. But that doesn’t always make it stop.\n\nSo yes, I do see that the doubts are often not… I don’t know, not proportionate, I guess. But in the moment they still feel important enough that I can’t quite let them go.\n\nDoctor: About how much time do these checking thoughts and mental reviewing take up in a typical day?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to put an exact number on it, because it’s not always like I sit down and check for one solid hour or something. It’s more broken up through the day. I’ll have a few minutes where I’m going over one thing, then I move on, then it pops back in again, and I’m mentally reviewing it while I’m trying to do something else.\n\nOn a typical day, I’d say maybe… I don’t know, maybe half an hour total? Sometimes more if it’s a busy day or if there were a lot of details to keep track of. It can feel like more than that because it keeps interrupting me. Like I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already did that,” but then the thought keeps looping back anyway. And then pretty soon I’ve gone from one concern to another, and I’m checking details in my head over and over.\n\nSo it’s not all day, exactly. I can still do my work and get through things. But it does take up enough time and attention that I notice it, and it wears on me.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts feel like unwanted intrusions, rather than the kind of worries you see as justified by real-life problems?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes and no. They do feel unwanted, in the sense that I’m not sitting there choosing to think about these things for fun. I’ll be trying to get through my day, and then suddenly I’m worrying about a work schedule, or whether I paid a bill, or whether I made some little paperwork error that’s going to come back and cause a bigger problem.\n\nBut at the same time, they don’t always feel completely random, if that makes sense. It’s usually tied to something real, like a bill or a travel plan or a detail at work. It’s just that my mind takes an ordinary thing and treats it like it could turn into some much larger mess. Like one small mix-up won’t just be inconvenient—it’ll somehow snowball. So part of me feels like, “Well, I’m being responsible, I should think about this,” and another part of me knows I’m going way past what the situation probably needs.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say they feel justified exactly. They feel like regular life worries that get too big and too sticky, and then I can’t quite put them down.\n\nDoctor: Besides going over things in your mind, do you also find yourself repeatedly checking things in a physical way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say repeatedly in a big way. I mean, I do normal checking, like making sure the door is locked or looking at an email before I send it, but I don’t think I’m going back over and over physically the way I do in my head.\n\nMost of it is more mental for me. Like I might remember that I already looked at something, but then I’ll keep replaying whether I looked carefully enough, if that makes sense. I’m not usually standing there checking the stove ten times or anything like that. It’s more that I’ll review it internally and sort of try to reassure myself that it was handled.\n\nI guess if I’m really unsure, I might look once more—like checking a bill payment confirmation or a calendar entry—but I don’t think it’s a major physical checking routine. It’s more the thinking afterward that gets sticky.\n\nDoctor: If you try not to go over it again in your mind, what does it feel like will happen?\n\nPatient: It feels like… if I don’t go over it, then I’m leaving something unfinished, even if I technically know I already handled it. Like there’s this uncomfortable loose end in my mind. I can tell myself, “Sunny, you already checked that, it’s fine, move on,” but it doesn’t really settle. The thought just sort of comes back around, like, “Are you sure? What if you missed one detail?”\n\nAnd then if I try to ignore it, I feel like I’m taking a risk, even though I know that sounds a little dramatic for the kinds of things I’m talking about. It’s not usually some huge crisis. It’s more like a schedule, a bill, a message, a small work detail. But my mind acts like if I don’t review it again, something could slip through and then I’ll have to deal with the consequences later.\n\nThe frustrating part is that it doesn’t stay with just one thing. I’ll start by trying not to think about one concern, and then another one pops up, and pretty soon I’m mentally checking several things. So even when I know it’s handled, the thought keeps looping, and I end up going over details in my head again just to try to feel sure.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries and mental checking start after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it did start after something specific. It’s been about nine months now, give or take, and it really seemed to begin after that overnight detention and the whole paperwork mix-up around it.\n\nI don’t know if I connected it right away, because at first I think I was just telling myself, “Well, of course I’m stressed, that was a bad experience,” and I was trying to just get back to normal. But when I look back, that’s when this pattern of second-guessing and going back over details really started to feel more constant. Before that, I could worry, sure, but it didn’t have this same… sticky quality to it.\n\nAnd the paperwork part of it especially, I think, made me feel like one mistake or one missing detail could turn into something much bigger than it should. So now when anything involves forms, schedules, bills, confirmations—anything where there’s a record or some official detail—I notice my mind goes right back to that feeling. But yes, the short answer is that it’s been going on for about nine months, since that incident and the paperwork mix-up connected to it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts ever feel like they pull you back into that detention incident, as if you’re reliving it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as reliving it, exactly. I mean, I do think about it, and sometimes the worry sort of points back to it, especially if something reminds me of paperwork or making sure something is documented correctly. But it’s more like I’m worrying about making another mistake or missing something, not like I’m suddenly back there in the moment.\n\nI don’t usually feel like I’m physically in that situation again, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that the memory kind of sits in the background and gives the current worry more weight. Like my mind says, “Remember what happened when something wasn’t handled right?” But I wouldn’t say I’m seeing it happen again or feeling like I’m actually there.\n\nSo… no, not really reliving it. More replaying the meaning of it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When you go over these details in your mind, does it feel like something you have to do to relieve the anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does feel like I have to do it, even when I know that doesn’t totally make sense. It’s like if I don’t go over the details, the anxiety just sits there and keeps poking at me. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already dealt with it,” but the thought doesn’t really listen to that. It keeps looping back around.\n\nAnd then I’ll start with one thing, like, “Did I send that message correctly?” or “Did I remember that appointment?” and before I know it, I’m onto another concern and then another one. It’s almost like my brain starts making a little list without me meaning to. So I end up checking details in my head over and over, trying to get that feeling of certainty.\n\nIt does relieve the anxiety a little bit, but only for a short time. Then the doubt creeps back in, and I feel pulled to review it again. That’s the part that bothers me, because I’m not doing it because I want to sit there thinking about all these things. I’m doing it because it feels like I won’t be able to relax unless I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid reminders of the detention incident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid reminders in a really clear way. I mean… I don’t think I’m going out of my way to avoid anything connected to it, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more that when something does remind me of it, I get uncomfortable and I start thinking more about whether everything is in order.\n\nLike, if there’s paperwork or official-looking forms or anything where I have to double-check details, I might feel that little tightening feeling, but I don’t think I’m avoiding those things entirely. I still deal with them if I have to. Maybe I’m a little slower with them, or I feel more tense, but I wouldn’t say I’m steering clear of reminders on purpose.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I haven’t really noticed avoidance so much as feeling uneasy when reminders come up.\n\nDoctor: While you’re doing the mental checking, does part of you feel it’s excessive or doesn’t really make sense?\n\nPatient: Yes, part of me knows it’s excessive. I can kind of hear myself thinking, Sunny, this is handled, you already checked it, you don’t need to keep going over it. But the problem is that knowing that doesn’t make the thought stop.\n\nIt’s like there’s the reasonable part of me that can see I’m circling, and then there’s the anxious part that keeps saying, But what if you missed something? What if you’re remembering it wrong? So I’ll try to move on, but then the thought loops right back around, and pretty soon I’ve gone from one concern to another. I might start out checking one detail in my head, and then suddenly I’m reviewing three or four other things too.\n\nSo yes, I do recognize that it doesn’t really make sense to keep doing it as much as I do. It just feels hard to stop once it gets going, because the checking feels like the only way to calm it down, even if only for a little while.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I don’t think it’s in a particular way, exactly. Not like I have to count things or repeat a phrase a certain number of times, or do it in some exact order until it feels perfect. It’s more… I get stuck reviewing it, but it’s not very organized.

It’s more that I keep circling until I feel a little calmer, or until I get tired of it, honestly.

So… if you mean, do I have a rule in my head like, “I have to think it through this exact way or it doesn’t count,” then no, I haven’t really noticed that. It’s more about trying to reassure myself that I didn’t miss anything.

It just feels hard to stop once it gets going, because the checking feels like the only way to calm it down, even if only for a little while.\n\nDoctor: When you go over things in your mind, do you feel you have to do it in a particular way until it feels just right?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s in a particular way, exactly. Not like I have to count things or repeat a phrase a certain number of times, or do it in some exact order until it feels perfect. It’s more… I get stuck reviewing it, but it’s not very organized.\n\nLike, I’ll go back over what happened or what I said or whether I forgot something, and I’m trying to get that settled feeling, but I don’t think there’s a special routine to it. Sometimes I’ll start in the middle of the thought, then jump backward, then jump to another detail, so it’s not like there’s one correct sequence I have to follow. It’s more that I keep circling until I feel a little calmer, or until I get tired of it, honestly.\n\nSo… if you mean, do I have a rule in my head like, “I have to think it through this exact way or it doesn’t count,” then no, I haven’t really noticed that. I’ll start thinking, “Did I forget to tell someone something?” or “What if I overlooked that note?” and then I lose where I was. Then I have to back up and check again, sometimes more than once, and it’s just been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts like “Did I forget to tell someone something?” pop up, do they feel intrusive and hard to dismiss?\n\nPatient: Yeah… intrusive is a good word for it, I think. It’s frustrating, because part of me knows I’ve already checked, or at least thought it through, but it still feels unsettled enough that I keep circling back.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeated doubts feel unreasonable to you, even while you’re having them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, a lot of the time I can tell it’s probably unreasonable, or at least bigger than the situation really calls for. Like, logically I know a small paperwork mistake at work is usually just a small mistake. Or if a schedule gets a little mixed up, it can usually be fixed. But that doesn’t always make it stop.\n\nSo yes, I do see that the doubts are often not… I don’t know, not proportionate, I guess. It’s more broken up through the day. I’ll have a few minutes where I’m going over one thing, then I move on, then it pops back in again, and I’m mentally reviewing it while I’m trying to do something else.\n\nOn a typical day, I’d say maybe… I don’t know, maybe half an hour total? Sometimes more if it’s a busy day or if there were a lot of details to keep track of. It can feel like more than that because it keeps interrupting me. Like I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already did that,” but then the thought keeps looping back anyway. And then pretty soon I’ve gone from one concern to another, and I’m checking details in my head over and over.\n\nSo it’s not all day, exactly. I can still do my work and get through things. But it does take up enough time and attention that I notice it, and it wears on me.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts feel like unwanted intrusions, rather than the kind of worries you see as justified by real-life problems?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes and no. They do feel unwanted, in the sense that I’m not sitting there choosing to think about these things for fun. I’ll be trying to get through my day, and then suddenly I’m worrying about a work schedule, or whether I paid a bill, or whether I made some little paperwork error that’s going to come back and cause a bigger problem.\n\nBut at the same time, they don’t always feel completely random, if that makes sense. It’s usually tied to something real, like a bill or a travel plan or a detail at work. It’s just that my mind takes an ordinary thing and treats it like it could turn into some much larger mess. Like one small mix-up won’t just be inconvenient—it’ll somehow snowball. So part of me feels like, “Well, I’m being responsible, I should think about this,” and another part of me knows I’m going way past what the situation probably needs.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say they feel justified exactly. They feel like regular life worries that get too big and too sticky, and then I can’t quite put them down.\n\nDoctor: Besides going over things in your mind, do you also find yourself repeatedly checking things in a physical way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say repeatedly in a big way. I mean, I do normal checking, like making sure the door is locked or looking at an email before I send it, but I don’t think I’m going back over and over physically the way I do in my head.\n\nMost of it is more mental for me. Like I might remember that I already looked at something, but then I’ll keep replaying whether I looked carefully enough, if that makes sense. I’m not usually standing there checking the stove ten times or anything like that. It’s more that I’ll review it internally and sort of try to reassure myself that it was handled.\n\nI guess if I’m really unsure, I might look once more—like checking a bill payment confirmation or a calendar entry—but I don’t think it’s a major physical checking routine. It’s more the thinking afterward that gets sticky.\n\nDoctor: If you try not to go over it again in your mind, what does it feel like will happen?\n\nPatient: It feels like… if I don’t go over it, then I’m leaving something unfinished, even if I technically know I already handled it. Like there’s this uncomfortable loose end in my mind. I can tell myself, “Sunny, you already checked that, it’s fine, move on,” but it doesn’t really settle. The thought just sort of comes back around, like, “Are you sure? It’s like if I don’t go over the details, the anxiety just sits there and keeps poking at me. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already dealt with it,” but the thought doesn’t really listen to that. It’s almost like my brain starts making a little list without me meaning to. So I end up checking details in my head over and over, trying to get that feeling of certainty.\n\nIt does relieve the anxiety a little bit, but only for a short time. Then the doubt creeps back in, and I feel pulled to review it again. That’s the part that bothers me, because I’m not doing it because I want to sit there thinking about all these things. I’m doing it because it feels like I won’t be able to relax unless I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid reminders of the detention incident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid reminders in a really clear way. I mean… I don’t think I’m going out of my way to avoid anything connected to it, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more that when something does remind me of it, I get uncomfortable and I start thinking more about whether everything is in order.\n\nLike, if there’s paperwork or official-looking forms or anything where I have to double-check details, I might feel that little tightening feeling, but I don’t think I’m avoiding those things entirely. I still deal with them if I have to. It’s more about trying to reassure myself that I didn’t miss anything.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself worrying excessively about many different parts of life, even when there isn’t a specific detail to check?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 44 / receptionist_or_information_clerk / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\nmanifestation: \"I find myself worrying about all kinds of ordinary things now—work schedules, bills, travel plans, even little paperwork errors—as if one small mix-up could turn into a much bigger problem.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I do. It’s not always tied to one specific thing I can point to and say, “This is what I need to check.” A lot of the time it’s more like my mind is scanning for what could go wrong.

It can be ordinary things, too, which is what feels frustrating. Like at work, I’ll worry about the schedule or whether I misunderstood something small. At home it might be bills, or whether I planned something correctly, or if there’s some little paperwork error I didn’t catch. Even travel plans—just basic things like times, reservations, directions—I can start imagining that one small mix-up is going to snowball into a much bigger problem.

And I know these are normal parts of life that everyone deals with, but I seem to give them more weight than they deserve. It’s like my mind treats a small loose end as if it could turn into a serious issue if I don’t stay on top of it. So yes, it’s definitely not just one detail. It spreads across a lot of different everyday things.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.9, F43.22, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess what finally made me make the appointment is that I’ve been having more trouble keeping my place with normal work things. I’m a receptionist, so I’m scheduling appointments and keeping track of little details all day, and lately I’ll be in the middle of entering something or arranging the lobby displays, and it’s like my mind just slips off track.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t know how to do the work or anything. I’ll start thinking, “Did I forget to tell someone something?” or “What if I overlooked that note?” and then I lose where I was. Then I have to back up and check again, sometimes more than once, and it’s just been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts like “Did I forget to tell someone something?” pop up, do they feel intrusive and hard to dismiss?\n\nPatient: Yeah… intrusive is a good word for it, I think. It’s like I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already did that,” and for maybe a second I believe it, but then the thought just sort of comes back around again. Like it doesn’t feel settled.\n\nAnd it’s not always just the one thing, either. I’ll start with, “Did I pass that message along?” and then it turns into, “What if I put the appointment in the wrong place?” or “What if I missed some little detail and someone’s going to be upset later?” And then I’m mentally going back through the steps, almost replaying them, checking them in my head over and over. I know it’s not really helping after a certain point, but it’s hard to just drop it and move on.\n\nDoctor: When those doubts come up, do you find yourself checking your work again and again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, but it’s a little tricky, because sometimes it’s not even that I physically go back and check the computer or the paper every single time. Sometimes I do that, definitely, like I’ll re-open the appointment or look back at a note I already know I read. But a lot of the time it’s more like I’m checking it in my head over and over.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already did that,” and I’ll try to move on, but the thought doesn’t really stay put. It loops back. And then I start going from one thing to the next, like first I’m worried I didn’t enter something correctly, then I’m thinking maybe I forgot to pass along a message, and then I’m remembering some tiny detail from earlier and wondering if I missed that too.\n\nSo yes, I guess I am checking again and again, just sometimes it’s mental checking more than anything. And it feels frustrating because I know after a point I’m not actually finding anything new, I’m just trying to get that settled feeling, and I don’t really get it.\n\nDoctor: When that unsettled feeling shows up, do you feel like you have to keep checking until it feels completely certain?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s pretty close to what happens. It’s not like I sit there thinking, “I need absolute certainty,” in those words, but that’s kind of what I’m reaching for. I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already did that,” and I do mean it in the moment. I’m trying to be reasonable with myself.\n\nBut then the thought doesn’t really stay gone. It loops back around, and then it’s like I’m not just checking the original thing anymore. I start with one concern, like whether I put something in correctly, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I passed along a message, or whether I missed some little note, or whether there was another detail I should have caught. And before I know it, I’m going through all these steps in my head over and over.\n\nI think I’m trying to get to that feeling of, “Okay, yes, everything is definitely fine,” but I don’t always get there. Or if I do, it doesn’t last very long. It’s frustrating, because part of me knows I’ve already checked, or at least thought it through, but it still feels unsettled enough that I keep circling back.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeated doubts feel unreasonable to you, even while you’re having them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, a lot of the time I can tell it’s probably unreasonable, or at least bigger than the situation really calls for. Like, logically I know a small paperwork mistake at work is usually just a small mistake. Or if a schedule gets a little mixed up, it can usually be fixed. But in my head it starts feeling like, “Well, what if this turns into a whole bigger problem?” Like one little thing could snowball somehow.\n\nAnd it’s not just work, honestly. I catch myself doing it with ordinary things too—bills, travel plans, schedules, little details that most people would probably check once and move on from. I’ll be thinking, “Did I pay that on time?” or “What if I misunderstood the plan?” or “What if I wrote something down wrong?” And even while I’m thinking it, there’s this part of me that knows, Sunny, you’re making this heavier than it needs to be. But that doesn’t always make it stop.\n\nSo yes, I do see that the doubts are often not… I don’t know, not proportionate, I guess. But in the moment they still feel important enough that I can’t quite let them go.\n\nDoctor: About how much time do these checking thoughts and mental reviewing take up in a typical day?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to put an exact number on it, because it’s not always like I sit down and check for one solid hour or something. It’s more broken up through the day. I’ll have a few minutes where I’m going over one thing, then I move on, then it pops back in again, and I’m mentally reviewing it while I’m trying to do something else.\n\nOn a typical day, I’d say maybe… I don’t know, maybe half an hour total? Sometimes more if it’s a busy day or if there were a lot of details to keep track of. It can feel like more than that because it keeps interrupting me. Like I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already did that,” but then the thought keeps looping back anyway. And then pretty soon I’ve gone from one concern to another, and I’m checking details in my head over and over.\n\nSo it’s not all day, exactly. I can still do my work and get through things. But it does take up enough time and attention that I notice it, and it wears on me.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts feel like unwanted intrusions, rather than the kind of worries you see as justified by real-life problems?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes and no. They do feel unwanted, in the sense that I’m not sitting there choosing to think about these things for fun. I’ll be trying to get through my day, and then suddenly I’m worrying about a work schedule, or whether I paid a bill, or whether I made some little paperwork error that’s going to come back and cause a bigger problem.\n\nBut at the same time, they don’t always feel completely random, if that makes sense. It’s usually tied to something real, like a bill or a travel plan or a detail at work. It’s just that my mind takes an ordinary thing and treats it like it could turn into some much larger mess. Like one small mix-up won’t just be inconvenient—it’ll somehow snowball. So part of me feels like, “Well, I’m being responsible, I should think about this,” and another part of me knows I’m going way past what the situation probably needs.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say they feel justified exactly. They feel like regular life worries that get too big and too sticky, and then I can’t quite put them down.\n\nDoctor: Besides going over things in your mind, do you also find yourself repeatedly checking things in a physical way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say repeatedly in a big way. I mean, I do normal checking, like making sure the door is locked or looking at an email before I send it, but I don’t think I’m going back over and over physically the way I do in my head.\n\nMost of it is more mental for me. Like I might remember that I already looked at something, but then I’ll keep replaying whether I looked carefully enough, if that makes sense. I’m not usually standing there checking the stove ten times or anything like that. It’s more that I’ll review it internally and sort of try to reassure myself that it was handled.\n\nI guess if I’m really unsure, I might look once more—like checking a bill payment confirmation or a calendar entry—but I don’t think it’s a major physical checking routine. It’s more the thinking afterward that gets sticky.\n\nDoctor: If you try not to go over it again in your mind, what does it feel like will happen?\n\nPatient: It feels like… if I don’t go over it, then I’m leaving something unfinished, even if I technically know I already handled it. Like there’s this uncomfortable loose end in my mind. I can tell myself, “Sunny, you already checked that, it’s fine, move on,” but it doesn’t really settle. The thought just sort of comes back around, like, “Are you sure? What if you missed one detail?”\n\nAnd then if I try to ignore it, I feel like I’m taking a risk, even though I know that sounds a little dramatic for the kinds of things I’m talking about. It’s not usually some huge crisis. It’s more like a schedule, a bill, a message, a small work detail. But my mind acts like if I don’t review it again, something could slip through and then I’ll have to deal with the consequences later.\n\nThe frustrating part is that it doesn’t stay with just one thing. I’ll start by trying not to think about one concern, and then another one pops up, and pretty soon I’m mentally checking several things. So even when I know it’s handled, the thought keeps looping, and I end up going over details in my head again just to try to feel sure.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries and mental checking start after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it did start after something specific. It’s been about nine months now, give or take, and it really seemed to begin after that overnight detention and the whole paperwork mix-up around it.\n\nI don’t know if I connected it right away, because at first I think I was just telling myself, “Well, of course I’m stressed, that was a bad experience,” and I was trying to just get back to normal. But when I look back, that’s when this pattern of second-guessing and going back over details really started to feel more constant. Before that, I could worry, sure, but it didn’t have this same… sticky quality to it.\n\nAnd the paperwork part of it especially, I think, made me feel like one mistake or one missing detail could turn into something much bigger than it should. So now when anything involves forms, schedules, bills, confirmations—anything where there’s a record or some official detail—I notice my mind goes right back to that feeling. But yes, the short answer is that it’s been going on for about nine months, since that incident and the paperwork mix-up connected to it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts ever feel like they pull you back into that detention incident, as if you’re reliving it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as reliving it, exactly. I mean, I do think about it, and sometimes the worry sort of points back to it, especially if something reminds me of paperwork or making sure something is documented correctly. But it’s more like I’m worrying about making another mistake or missing something, not like I’m suddenly back there in the moment.\n\nI don’t usually feel like I’m physically in that situation again, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that the memory kind of sits in the background and gives the current worry more weight. Like my mind says, “Remember what happened when something wasn’t handled right?” But I wouldn’t say I’m seeing it happen again or feeling like I’m actually there.\n\nSo… no, not really reliving it. More replaying the meaning of it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When you go over these details in your mind, does it feel like something you have to do to relieve the anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does feel like I have to do it, even when I know that doesn’t totally make sense. It’s like if I don’t go over the details, the anxiety just sits there and keeps poking at me. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already dealt with it,” but the thought doesn’t really listen to that. It keeps looping back around.\n\nAnd then I’ll start with one thing, like, “Did I send that message correctly?” or “Did I remember that appointment?” and before I know it, I’m onto another concern and then another one. It’s almost like my brain starts making a little list without me meaning to. So I end up checking details in my head over and over, trying to get that feeling of certainty.\n\nIt does relieve the anxiety a little bit, but only for a short time. Then the doubt creeps back in, and I feel pulled to review it again. That’s the part that bothers me, because I’m not doing it because I want to sit there thinking about all these things. I’m doing it because it feels like I won’t be able to relax unless I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid reminders of the detention incident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid reminders in a really clear way. I mean… I don’t think I’m going out of my way to avoid anything connected to it, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more that when something does remind me of it, I get uncomfortable and I start thinking more about whether everything is in order.\n\nLike, if there’s paperwork or official-looking forms or anything where I have to double-check details, I might feel that little tightening feeling, but I don’t think I’m avoiding those things entirely. I still deal with them if I have to. Maybe I’m a little slower with them, or I feel more tense, but I wouldn’t say I’m steering clear of reminders on purpose.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I haven’t really noticed avoidance so much as feeling uneasy when reminders come up.\n\nDoctor: While you’re doing the mental checking, does part of you feel it’s excessive or doesn’t really make sense?\n\nPatient: Yes, part of me knows it’s excessive. I can kind of hear myself thinking, Sunny, this is handled, you already checked it, you don’t need to keep going over it. But the problem is that knowing that doesn’t make the thought stop.\n\nIt’s like there’s the reasonable part of me that can see I’m circling, and then there’s the anxious part that keeps saying, But what if you missed something? What if you’re remembering it wrong? So I’ll try to move on, but then the thought loops right back around, and pretty soon I’ve gone from one concern to another. I might start out checking one detail in my head, and then suddenly I’m reviewing three or four other things too.\n\nSo yes, I do recognize that it doesn’t really make sense to keep doing it as much as I do. It just feels hard to stop once it gets going, because the checking feels like the only way to calm it down, even if only for a little while.\n\nDoctor: When you go over things in your mind, do you feel you have to do it in a particular way until it feels just right?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s in a particular way, exactly. Not like I have to count things or repeat a phrase a certain number of times, or do it in some exact order until it feels perfect. It’s more… I get stuck reviewing it, but it’s not very organized.\n\nLike, I’ll go back over what happened or what I said or whether I forgot something, and I’m trying to get that settled feeling, but I don’t think there’s a special routine to it. Sometimes I’ll start in the middle of the thought, then jump backward, then jump to another detail, so it’s not like there’s one correct sequence I have to follow. It’s more that I keep circling until I feel a little calmer, or until I get tired of it, honestly.\n\nSo… if you mean, do I have a rule in my head like, “I have to think it through this exact way or it doesn’t count,” then no, I haven’t really noticed that. It’s more about trying to reassure myself that I didn’t miss anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different parts of life, even when there isn’t a specific detail to check?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s not always tied to one specific thing I can point to and say, “This is what I need to check.” A lot of the time it’s more like my mind is scanning for what could go wrong.\n\nIt can be ordinary things, too, which is what feels frustrating. Like at work, I’ll worry about the schedule or whether I misunderstood something small. At home it might be bills, or whether I planned something correctly, or if there’s some little paperwork error I didn’t catch. Even travel plans—just basic things like times, reservations, directions—I can start imagining that one small mix-up is going to snowball into a much bigger problem.\n\nAnd I know these are normal parts of life that everyone deals with, but I seem to give them more weight than they deserve. It spreads across a lot of different everyday things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s more that I’ll review it internally and sort of try to reassure myself that it was handled.\n\nI guess if I’m really unsure, I might look once more—like checking a bill payment confirmation or a calendar entry—but I don’t think it’s a major physical checking routine. It’s more the thinking afterward that gets sticky.\n\nDoctor: If you try not to go over it again in your mind, what does it feel like will happen?\n\nPatient: It feels like… if I don’t go over it, then I’m leaving something unfinished, even if I technically know I already handled it. Like there’s this uncomfortable loose end in my mind. I can tell myself, “Sunny, you already checked that, it’s fine, move on,” but it doesn’t really settle. The thought just sort of comes back around, like, “Are you sure? What if you missed one detail?”\n\nAnd then if I try to ignore it, I feel like I’m taking a risk, even though I know that sounds a little dramatic for the kinds of things I’m talking about. It’s not usually some huge crisis. It’s more like a schedule, a bill, a message, a small work detail. But my mind acts like if I don’t review it again, something could slip through and then I’ll have to deal with the consequences later.\n\nThe frustrating part is that it doesn’t stay with just one thing. I’ll start by trying not to think about one concern, and then another one pops up, and pretty soon I’m mentally checking several things. So even when I know it’s handled, the thought keeps looping, and I end up going over details in my head again just to try to feel sure.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries and mental checking start after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it did start after something specific. It’s been about nine months now, give or take, and it really seemed to begin after that overnight detention and the whole paperwork mix-up around it.\n\nI don’t know if I connected it right away, because at first I think I was just telling myself, “Well, of course I’m stressed, that was a bad experience,” and I was trying to just get back to normal. But when I look back, that’s when this pattern of second-guessing and going back over details really started to feel more constant. Before that, I could worry, sure, but it didn’t have this same… sticky quality to it.\n\nAnd the paperwork part of it especially, I think, made me feel like one mistake or one missing detail could turn into something much bigger than it should. So now when anything involves forms, schedules, bills, confirmations—anything where there’s a record or some official detail—I notice my mind goes right back to that feeling. But yes, the short answer is that it’s been going on for about nine months, since that incident and the paperwork mix-up connected to it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts ever feel like they pull you back into that detention incident, as if you’re reliving it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as reliving it, exactly. I mean, I do think about it, and sometimes the worry sort of points back to it, especially if something reminds me of paperwork or making sure something is documented correctly. But it’s more like I’m worrying about making another mistake or missing something, not like I’m suddenly back there in the moment.\n\nI don’t usually feel like I’m physically in that situation again, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that the memory kind of sits in the background and gives the current worry more weight. Like my mind says, “Remember what happened when something wasn’t handled right?” But I wouldn’t say I’m seeing it happen again or feeling like I’m actually there.\n\nSo… no, not really reliving it. More replaying the meaning of it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When you go over these details in your mind, does it feel like something you have to do to relieve the anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does feel like I have to do it, even when I know that doesn’t totally make sense. It’s like if I don’t go over the details, the anxiety just sits there and keeps poking at me. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already dealt with it,” but the thought doesn’t really listen to that. It keeps looping back around.\n\nAnd then I’ll start with one thing, like, “Did I send that message correctly?” or “Did I remember that appointment?” and before I know it, I’m onto another concern and then another one. It’s almost like my brain starts making a little list without me meaning to. So I end up checking details in my head over and over, trying to get that feeling of certainty.\n\nIt does relieve the anxiety a little bit, but only for a short time. Then the doubt creeps back in, and I feel pulled to review it again. That’s the part that bothers me, because I’m not doing it because I want to sit there thinking about all these things. I’m doing it because it feels like I won’t be able to relax unless I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid reminders of the detention incident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid reminders in a really clear way. I mean… I don’t think I’m going out of my way to avoid anything connected to it, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more that when something does remind me of it, I get uncomfortable and I start thinking more about whether everything is in order.\n\nLike, if there’s paperwork or official-looking forms or anything where I have to double-check details, I might feel that little tightening feeling, but I don’t think I’m avoiding those things entirely. I still deal with them if I have to. Maybe I’m a little slower with them, or I feel more tense, but I wouldn’t say I’m steering clear of reminders on purpose.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I haven’t really noticed avoidance so much as feeling uneasy when reminders come up.\n\nDoctor: While you’re doing the mental checking, does part of you feel it’s excessive or doesn’t really make sense?\n\nPatient: Yes, part of me knows it’s excessive. I can kind of hear myself thinking, Sunny, this is handled, you already checked it, you don’t need to keep going over it. But the problem is that knowing that doesn’t make the thought stop.\n\nIt’s like there’s the reasonable part of me that can see I’m circling, and then there’s the anxious part that keeps saying, But what if you missed something? What if you’re remembering it wrong? So I’ll try to move on, but then the thought loops right back around, and pretty soon I’ve gone from one concern to another. I might start out checking one detail in my head, and then suddenly I’m reviewing three or four other things too.\n\nSo yes, I do recognize that it doesn’t really make sense to keep doing it as much as I do. It just feels hard to stop once it gets going, because the checking feels like the only way to calm it down, even if only for a little while.\n\nDoctor: When you go over things in your mind, do you feel you have to do it in a particular way until it feels just right?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s in a particular way, exactly. Not like I have to count things or repeat a phrase a certain number of times, or do it in some exact order until it feels perfect. It’s more… I get stuck reviewing it, but it’s not very organized.\n\nLike, I’ll go back over what happened or what I said or whether I forgot something, and I’m trying to get that settled feeling, but I don’t think there’s a special routine to it. Sometimes I’ll start in the middle of the thought, then jump backward, then jump to another detail, so it’s not like there’s one correct sequence I have to follow. It’s more that I keep circling until I feel a little calmer, or until I get tired of it, honestly.\n\nSo… if you mean, do I have a rule in my head like, “I have to think it through this exact way or it doesn’t count,” then no, I haven’t really noticed that. It’s more about trying to reassure myself that I didn’t miss anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different parts of life, even when there isn’t a specific detail to check?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s not always tied to one specific thing I can point to and say, “This is what I need to check.” A lot of the time it’s more like my mind is scanning for what could go wrong.\n\nIt can be ordinary things, too, which is what feels frustrating. Like at work, I’ll worry about the schedule or whether I misunderstood something small. At home it might be bills, or whether I planned something correctly, or if there’s some little paperwork error I didn’t catch. Even travel plans—just basic things like times, reservations, directions—I can start imagining that one small mix-up is going to snowball into a much bigger problem.\n\nAnd I know these are normal parts of life that everyone deals with, but I seem to give them more weight than they deserve. It’s like my mind treats a small loose end as if it could turn into a serious issue if I don’t stay on top of it. So yes, it’s definitely not just one detail. It spreads across a lot of different everyday things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes, I do. Even when I’m not sitting there deliberately reviewing something, the worry still feels hard to shut off. I can tell myself, Sunny, it’s handled, there’s nothing else to do right now, but the thought doesn’t really listen to that.

Then I have to back up and check again, sometimes more than once, and it’s just been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts like “Did I forget to tell someone something?” pop up, do they feel intrusive and hard to dismiss?\n\nPatient: Yeah… intrusive is a good word for it, I think. I know it’s not really helping after a certain point, but it’s hard to just drop it and move on.\n\nDoctor: When those doubts come up, do you find yourself checking your work again and again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, but it’s a little tricky, because sometimes it’s not even that I physically go back and check the computer or the paper every single time.
